    The other diagonal from d6 is d6-c5-b4-a3.
    And d6-e7-f8.
    And d6-c7-b8.
    So the Bishop ONLY attacks the h2-d6 diagonal.
    If I move h2 to h3, it's no longer on that diagonal!
    So h2h3 actually removes the pawn from the Bishop's potential line (once the e5 pawn moves).
    This is a very strong positional move.

    *   Current: Pawn at h2 is on the d6-h2 diagonal. Blocked by e5.
    *   Move h2h3: Pawn is now at h3. Is h3 on the d6 diagonal?
        d6: (6, 4)
        h3: (3, 8)
        Difference: 6-3=3, 4-8=-4. Not a diagonal.
    *   Move h2h4: Pawn is now at h4. Is h4 on the d6 diagonal?
        d6: (6, 4)
        h4: (4, 8)
        Difference: 6-4=2, 4-8=-4. Not a diagonal.
    *   Move g2g3: Pawn is now at g3. Is g3 on the d6 diagonal?
        d6: (6, 4)
        g3: (3, 7)
        Difference: 6-3=3, 4-7=-3. YES.
        So if I move g2g3, and the pawn at e5 moves, the Bishop at d6 will attack g3.

    Therefore, h2h3 or h2h4 are better than g2g3.
    Between h2h3 and h2h4, h2h3 is more conservative and keeps the pawn’s flexibility.

    Actually, let's consider the King moves.
    f2e3: Moves toward the center.
    f2e2: Stays back.
    f2f3: Moves up.
    f2g3: Moves onto the diagonal (as discussed, will be in check if e5 moves).

    h2h3 seems like a very safe and useful move. It removes the pawn from the Bishop's potential line and prepares to stabilize the position.

    Wait, let me re-verify the coordinates one more time.
    Bishop d6 (Row 6, Col 4)
    h2 (Row 2, Col 8)
    6-2 = 4
    4-8 = -4
    Yes, diagonal.
    h3 (Row 3, Col 8)
    6-3 = 3
    4-8 = -4
    Not diagonal.
    g3 (Row 3, Col 7)
    6-3 = 3
    4-7 = -3
    Yes, diagonal.
    h4 (Row 4, Col 8)
    6-4 = 2
    4-8 = -4
    Not diagonal.

    So h2h3 is a good move. Let's go with that.
